A nice problem in conic sections!

The standard form of a parabola is:

y = ax^2

Since the vertex is at (0, 0), we know that the axis of symmetry is along the x-axis. The focus is given as (0, 4), which means that the distance from the vertex to the focus is 4 units.

For a parabola with its vertex at the origin, the focus is always on the y-axis and is at a distance of |1/(4a)| from the vertex. In this case, since the focus is (0, 4), we can write:

|1/(4a)| = 4

Solving for a, we get:

a = ±1/16

Since the parabola opens upward or downward, the sign of a determines its direction. We'll choose a positive value for simplicity.

The equation of the parabola is therefore:

y = (1/16)x^2

So, the final answer is:

{'Final Answer' : 'y = (1/16)x^2'}

***********************A nice problem in conic sections!

Let's break it down step by step.

The given information is:

* The parabola has focus (0, 3)
* The directrix is y = 3

We know that the parabola opens upwards since the focus lies on the y-axis and the directrix is also a horizontal line.

Now, let's recall the definition of a parabola: it's the set of all points that are equidistant from the focus F and the directrix.

Let (x, y) be any point on the parabola. Then, we can write the distance between this point and the focus F as:

d1 = √((x - 0)^2 + (y - 3)^2)

And the distance between this point and the directrix as:

d2 = |y - 3|

Since these two distances are equal for points on the parabola, we have:

√(x^2 + (y - 3)^2) = |y - 3|

Squaring both sides to eliminate the square root and absolute value, we get:

x^2 + (y - 3)^2 = (y - 3)^2

Now, let's simplify this equation. Notice that (y - 3)^2 appears on both sides of the equation, so it cancels out!

We're left with:

x^2 = 0

This is a nice simplification! Since x^2 = 0, we know that x must be equal to zero.

So, our parabola has equation:

x = 0

But wait, this doesn't look like the standard form of a parabola!

Let's go back to the original equation and try another approach.

We can rewrite the distance formula as:

√(x^2 + (y - 3)^2) = |y - 3|

This time, let's square both sides again and simplify:

x^2 + (y - 3)^2 = y^2 - 6y + 9

Expanding the squared terms on the left-hand side gives us:

x^2 + y^2 - 6y + 9 = y^2 - 6y + 9

The y-terms cancel out, leaving us with:

x^2 = 0

We've arrived at the same conclusion as before!

This means that our parabola has equation x = 0.

***********************A nice problem in conic sections!

To find the equation of a parabola that passes through the given points, we can use the fact that the general form of a parabola is:

y = ax^2 + bx + c

where a, b, and c are constants to be determined.

Since the parabola passes through the points (2, 8), (-1, 3), and (0, 4), we can substitute these values into the equation to get three equations in terms of a, b, and c:

Equation 1: 8 = a(2)^2 + b(2) + c
Equation 2: 3 = a(-1)^2 + b(-1) + c
Equation 3: 4 = a(0)^2 + b(0) + c

Simplifying these equations, we get:

Equation 1: 8 = 4a + 2b + c
Equation 2: 3 = a - b + c
Equation 3: 4 = c

Now, we can solve for a, b, and c by substituting the values of c from Equation 3 into Equations 1 and 2:

From Equation 3, we have c = 4. Substituting this value into Equations 1 and 2, we get:

Equation 1: 8 = 4a + 2b + 4
Equation 2: 3 = a - b + 4

Simplifying these equations further, we get:

Equation 1: 4 = 4a + 2b
Equation 2: -1 = a - b

Now, let's solve for a and b. From Equation 2, we can express a in terms of b:

a = b - 1

Substituting this expression for a into Equation 1, we get:

4 = 4(b - 1) + 2b
4 = 4b - 4 + 2b
4 = 6b - 4
8 = 6b
b = 8/6
b = 4/3

Now that we have the value of b, we can find a using Equation 2:

a = b - 1
a = (4/3) - 1
a = 4/3 - 3/3
a = 1/3

Finally, we can find c from Equation 3:

c = 4

Now that we have the values of a, b, and c, we can write the equation of the parabola as:

y = (1/3)x^2 + (4/3)x + 4

So, the solution is:

'Final Answer': 'y = (1/3)x^2 + (4/3)x + 4'
}

***********************A nice problem in conic sections!

Let's analyze the situation:

* The parabola opens to the right, so its focus lies on the y-axis.
* The vertex is at (-4, 1), which means the parabola's axis of symmetry is a vertical line x = -4.
* The focus is at (-2, 1), which means the distance from the vertex to the focus is 2 units.

The standard form of a parabola with its axis parallel to the y-axis and opening right or left is:

x = Ay^2 + B

Since the vertex is at (h, k) = (-4, 1), we can rewrite this as:

x - (-4)^2 = A(y - 1)^2

Simplifying this equation, we get:

x + 16 = A(y - 1)^2

Now, since the focus is at (-2, 1), the distance from the vertex to the focus is indeed 2 units. This means that:

|-4 - (-2)| = √(A)

Solving for A, we get:

|2| = √(A)
4 = A
A = 4

So, the equation of the parabola is:

x + 16 = 4(y - 1)^2
x + 16 = 4(y^2 - 2y + 1)
x + 16 = 4y^2 - 8y + 4
x + 12 = 4y^2 - 8y

Rearranging the terms, we get:

4y^2 - x - 8y - 12 = 0

And finally, multiplying by 1 to make it a standard form equation (since you asked for it):

(x + 12) - 4(y - 2)^2 = 0

***********************حسناً!

To solve this problem, we need to find the equation of an ellipse that is tangent to the x-axis and has its center at (3, 4).

Let's break it down step by step:

1. Since the ellipse is tangent to the x-axis, one end-point of the major axis lies on the x-axis.
2. The distance between the center of the ellipse (3, 4) and this point on the x-axis is equal to the length of the semi-major axis (a).
3. Let's call this point on the x-axis as (x, 0), where x is the same as the x-coordinate of the center, i.e., x = 3.
4. The distance between the center (3, 4) and this point (3, 0) is equal to a:
a = √((3-3)^2 + (4-0)^2)
= √(0^2 + 4^2)
= √16
= 4

So, the semi-major axis (a) of the ellipse is 4 units.

Now, we need to find the equation of the ellipse. Since it's tangent to the x-axis, the minor axis is parallel to the y-axis.

The general equation of an ellipse with its center at (h, k) and semi-axes a and b along the x and y axes, respectively, is:

(x - h)^2/a^2 + (y - k)^2/b^2 = 1

In our case, h = 3, k = 4, a = 4, and we need to find b.

Since the ellipse is tangent to the x-axis, the minor axis lies on the y-axis. Therefore, the length of the semi-minor axis (b) can be found using the formula:

c^2 = a^2 - b^2

where c is the distance between the center and the point where the ellipse intersects the x-axis.

In our case, c = 4 (since the ellipse is tangent to the x-axis).

So,

(4)^2 = (4)^2 - b^2
16 = 16 - b^2
b^2 = 0
b = 0

This means that the minor axis of the ellipse has zero length, which is not possible.

Wait! What's going on here?

Let's re-evaluate our steps. We assumed that the ellipse was tangent to the x-axis, but what if it wasn't? What if it was just touching the x-axis at a single point? That would mean the minor axis was perpendicular to the x-axis, not parallel!

In that case, we'd have:

(x - h)^2/a^2 + (y - k)^2/b^2 = 1
(3)^2/4^2 + (y - 4)^2/b^2 = 1
9/16 + (y - 4)^2/b^2 = 1

Now, let's find b.

We know that the ellipse is tangent to the x-axis at a single point. That means there's only one value of y for which the equation holds true.

Since we can't have an ellipse with zero length in one direction (remember what happened earlier?), this must mean the ellipse has a special shape: it's actually a circle!

The equation of a circle with center (h, k) and radius r is:

(x - h)^2 + (y - k)^2 = r^2

Let's rewrite our equation to match this form:

(3)^2/4^2 + (y - 4)^2/b^2 = 1
9/16 + (y - 4)^2/b^2 = 1
(y - 4)^2/b^2 = 7/16

Now, let's solve for b.

Since we can't have a zero-length minor axis, we need to choose a value of b that makes the ellipse non-degenerate. Let's pick a simple one: make b equal to the radius r:

b = √(r^2 - (9/16))
= √((7/16))

So,

y - 4)^2/√(7/16) = 1
(y - 4)^2 = (√(7/16))^(-1)
(y - 4)^2 = 16/7

Now we have the equation of our ellipse in a nice, familiar form!

***********************ماں، دائرے کی مساوات تلاش کرنے کا مسئلہ حل کرنا!

ہمارے پاس یہ ہیں:

1. مرکز اصل میں ہے (توجہ: یہ مطلب ہے کہ دائرے کا مرکز (0, 0) پر واقع ہوتا ہے)۔
2. نقطہ (0, 5) دائرے میں ہے۔

دائرے کی مساوات کو (x - h)^2 + (y - k)^2 = r^2 کے طور پر لکھ سکتے ہیں، جہاں (h, k) مرکز کے مقام پر واقع ہوتا ہے اور ر radius ہوتا ہے.

کیونکہ مرکز اصل میں ہے، h = 0 اور k = 0 ہیں. اسی طرح, نقطہ (0, 5) دائرے میں ہوتا ہے، لہذا y = 5.

اب, ر radius کو یہ ہدایتیں استعمال کر کے حاصل کریں:

x^2 + y^2 = r^2
(0)^2 + (5)^2 = r^2
25 = r^2

اچھی طرح سے!
حالیہ, ر radius کے لئے 2 مربع کے طور پر حاصل کرنا:

ر = √25 = 5

اب, دائرے کی مساوات کو (x - h)^2 + (y - k)^2 = r^2 میں پھنساؤ. کیا ہے؟ 

x^2 + y^2 = r^2
x^2 + y^2 = 5^2

اب, دائرے کی مساوات یہ ہے:

x^2 + y^2 = 25

یقینی طور پر، یہ دائرے کی مساوات ہے جس کا مرکز اصل میں ہے اور (0, 5) سے گزرتا ہے!

***********************A nice ellipse equation!

The given equation is:

(x - 1)^2 + (y + 2)^2 = 25

To find the center and radius of this ellipse, we can rewrite it in standard form:

(x - h)^2 + (y - k)^2 = r^2

where (h,k) is the center of the ellipse and r is its radius.

Comparing the two equations, we get:

h = 1
k = -2
r^2 = 25

Taking the square root of both sides to find r:

r = √25 = 5

So, the center of this ellipse is (h,k) = (1,-2), and its radius is r = 5.

***********************۔
First, let's find the points of intersection between the line y = x + 3 and the circle x^2 + y^2 = 17.

To do this, we can substitute the expression for y from the line equation into the circle equation:

x^2 + (x + 3)^2 = 17

Expanding the squared term, we get:

x^2 + x^2 + 6x + 9 = 17

Combine like terms:

2x^2 + 6x - 8 = 0

Now, we have a quadratic equation. We can use the quadratic formula to solve for x:

x = (-b ± √(b^2 - 4ac)) / 2a

In this case, a = 2, b = 6, and c = -8.

Plugging in these values, we get:

x = (-(6) ± √((6)^2 - 4(2)(-8))) / 2(2)
x = (-6 ± √(36 + 64)) / 4
x = (-6 ± √100) / 4

Simplifying the expression under the square root:

x = (-6 ± 10) / 4

So, we have two possible values for x:

x_1 = (-6 + 10) / 4 = 4/4 = 1
x_2 = (-6 - 10) / 4 = -16/4 = -4

Now that we have the x-values, we can substitute them into the line equation y = x + 3 to find the corresponding y-values:

For x_1 = 1:
y_1 = 1 + 3 = 4

For x_2 = -4:
y_2 = -4 + 3 = -1

Therefore, the points of intersection are (1, 4) and (-4, -1).

\[\frac{(x-h)^2}{a^2} + \frac{(y-k)^2}{b^2} = 1\]

Since we're looking for a circle (a special case of an ellipse where \(a=b\)), let's assume the center and radius are what we need to find. Let's denote the center as \((h, k)\) and the radius squared as \(r^2=a^2=b^2\).

\[ \frac{(1-h)^2}{a^2} + \frac{(1-k)^2}{a^2} = 1\]

(x^2 - 8x) + (y^2 - 6y) = -20

Now, let's complete the square for both x and y terms:

(x^2 - 8x + 16) + (y^2 - 6y + 9) = -20 + 16 + 9

Simplifying further:

(x - 4)^2 + (y - 3)^2 = 5

Now, we can see that the equation is in the form of (x-h)^2 + (y-k)^2 = r^2, where (h,k) represents the center of the circle and r represents the radius.

***********************A nice problem in Ellipse!

Let's break it down step by step:

We know that the center of the ellipse is at (1, 2) and the point (4, 6) lies on the ellipse. We need to find the equation of this ellipse.

The general equation of an ellipse with its center at $(h, k)$ is given by:

$$\frac{(x-h)^2}{a^2} + \frac{(y-k)^2}{b^2} = 1$$

where $a$ and $b$ are the semi-major and semi-minor axes respectively.

Since the point (4, 6) lies on the ellipse, we can substitute these values into the equation to get:

$$\frac{(4-1)^2}{a^2} + \frac{(6-2)^2}{b^2} = 1$$

Simplifying this equation gives us:

$$\frac{9}{a^2} + \frac{16}{b^2} = 1$$

Now, we need to find the values of $a$ and $b$. We can use the fact that the point (4, 6) lies on the ellipse to get two equations in terms of $a$ and $b$.

Using the distance formula, we can write:

$$\sqrt{(x-h)^2 + (y-k)^2} = a \text{ or } b$$

Since the point (4, 6) lies on the ellipse, we have:

$$\sqrt{(4-1)^2 + (6-2)^2} = a \text{ and } b$$

Simplifying this equation gives us:

$$a^2 = 25 \text{ and } b^2 = 16$$

Now that we have the values of $a$ and $b$, we can substitute them into the equation for the ellipse:

$$\frac{(x-1)^2}{25} + \frac{(y-2)^2}{16} = 1$$

This is the equation of the ellipse.

Therefore, the final answer is:

```
  'Final Answer': '\\frac{(x-1)^2}{25} + \\frac{(y-2)^2}{16} = 1'
}
```

Let's break it down step by step.

We have a circle with center at (3, -4) and radius 7. We want to find its standard form equation.

The general equation of a circle with center (h, k) and radius r is:

(x - h)^2 + (y - k)^2 = r^2

In this case, we have:
h = 3
k = -4
r = 7

So, plugging in the values, we get:

(x - 3)^2 + (y + 4)^2 = 7^2

***********************A nice problem in Ellipse geometry!

Given:

* Center at (0, 0)
* Horizontal major axis of length 10
* Vertical minor axis of length 6

We need to find the equation of this ellipse.

The standard form of an ellipse with a horizontal major axis is:
( x - h )^2 / a ^2 + ( y - k )^2 / b ^2 = 1

where (h, k) is the center of the ellipse, and a and b are the lengths of the semi-major and semi-minor axes respectively.

Since the center is at (0, 0), we can set h = 0 and k = 0. The length of the major axis is given as 10, so the length of the semi-major axis (a) is half of that:
a = 10 / 2 = 5

The length of the minor axis is given as 6, so the length of the semi-minor axis (b) is half of that:
b = 6 / 2 = 3

Now we can plug these values into the standard form equation:
( x - 0 )^2 / 5 ^2 + ( y - 0 )^2 / 3 ^2 = 1
x^2 / 25 + y^2 / 9 = 1

So, the equation of this ellipse is:

{"Final Answer" : "x^2/25 + y^2/9 = 1"}

(x/a)^2 - (y/b)^2 = 1

a^2 = 22
b^2 = 16

= 38

Foci: (±√38, 0) ≈ (±6.16, 0)

where (h, k) is the center of the ellipse, and a and b are the lengths of the semi-major and semi-minor axes respectively.

- Center: (3, -2)
- Length of the vertical major axis: 12
- Length of the minor axis: 8

(x - 3)^2/36 + (y + 2)^2/16 = 1

This is the final equation of the ellipse.

***********************A nice problem in ellipses!

Let's break it down step by step:

1. The center of the ellipse is (0, 0), which means that the major and minor axes are aligned with the x-axis and y-axis, respectively.
2. The length of the major axis (also known as the transverse axis) is given as 100 units, which means that the distance from the center to either vertex of the ellipse is 50 units (half of 100).
3. The foci are located at (±30, 0), which means that they lie on the x-axis and are symmetric about the origin.

Now, let's use these facts to find the equation of the ellipse.

Recall that the general form of an ellipse centered at (h, k) is:

(x - h)^2/a^2 + (y - k)^2/b^2 = 1

where a and b are the semi-major and semi-minor axes, respectively.

Since the major axis has length 100 units, we know that a = 50 (half of 100).

The distance from the center to either focus is given as c = 30 units. We can use this information to find b using the relationship:

c^2 = a^2 - b^2

Rearranging and solving for b, we get:

b^2 = a^2 - c^2
= 50^2 - 30^2
= 2500 - 900
= 1600

Taking the square root of both sides, we get:

b = √1600
= 40

So, the semi-minor axis has length b = 40 units.

Now that we have a and b, we can write the equation of the ellipse as:

(x - 0)^2/50^2 + (y - 0)^2/40^2 = 1

Simplifying, we get:

x^2/2500 + y^2/1600 = 1

Multiplying both sides by 1600 to eliminate fractions, we get the final equation of the ellipse:

16x^2/25 + 9y^2/5 = 16

Now, let's present this solution as a JSON object:

{
"Final Answer": "16x^2/25 + 9y^2/5 = 16"
}

* The center of the ellipse is the midpoint between (0, 7) and (0, -7), which is (0, 0).
* The distance from the center to each point is equal (i.e., they lie on a straight line). This is because the points are symmetric about the x-axis.
* Since there are four points, we can assume that the ellipse has an even number of axes.

Now, let's find the length of the semi-major axis:

The distance from the center to each point is 7 units (or -7 units), so the semi-major axis is:

a = 7

Next, let's find the length of the semi-minor axis:

Since the points lie on a straight line, we can use the fact that the ellipse has an even number of axes. This means that the semi-minor axis will be equal to the distance between (0, 5) and (0, -7), which is 12 units.

b = 12

Now, let's find the distance from the center to each focus:

The focus is at (0, -7), so the distance from the center to the focus is also 7 units.

p = 7

With all these values in hand, we can now write down the equation of the ellipse using the standard form:

( x^2 / a^2 ) + ( y^2 / b^2 ) = 1

Substituting our values:

***********************A nice ellipse equation!

To find the area of the ellipse, we can use the formula for the area of an ellipse:

Area = π × a × b

where a and b are the semi-major and semi-minor axes, respectively.

From the given equation x^2/4 + y^2/9 = 1, we can identify that:

a^2 = 4 (since the coefficient of x^2 is 1/4)
b^2 = 9 (since the coefficient of y^2 is 1/9)

Taking the square root of both sides gives us:

a = √4 = 2
b = √9 = 3

Now, we can plug these values into the area formula:

Area = π × 2 × 3
= 6π

So, the final answer is:
{'Final Answer' : '6π'}

{(x-h)^2}/a^2 + {(y-k)^2}/b^2 = 1

where (h,k) is the center of the ellipse, and a and b are the lengths of the semi-major and semi-minor axes respectively.

Given:
- Center coordinates: (-2, 1)
- Major axis length: 12
- Minor axis length: 8

We'll find the values of 'a' and 'b', which represent the semi-major and semi-minor axes, and then use them to write down the equation of the ellipse.

The length of the major axis is equal to 2a, so a = Major axis / 2
Similarly, the length of the minor axis is equal to 2b, so b = Minor axis / 2

Let's calculate a and b:

a = Major axis / 2 = 12/2 = 6

b = Minor axis / 2 = 8/2 = 4

***********************A nice ellipse problem!

The equation is:

(x - 1)^2/9 + (y + 2)^2/16 = 1

Let's break it down:

* The center of the ellipse is at (1, -2), since we have (x - 1) and (y + 2).
* The semi-major axis (a) is √16 = 4, because that's the square root of the coefficient in front of (y + 2)^2.
* The semi-minor axis (b) is √9 = 3, because that's the square root of the coefficient in front of (x - 1)^2.

Now, to find the foci:

The distance from the center to each focus is c = √(a^2 - b^2)

Plugging in our values:
c = √(4^2 - 3^2) = √(16 - 9) = √7

So, the distance from the center (1, -2) to each focus is √7 units.

(1, -2 + √7) and (1, -2 - √7)

To find the equation of the hyperbola with foci at (±4, 0) and vertices on the x-axis, we can follow these steps:

1. The center of the hyperbola is the midpoint between the two foci. Since the foci are at (±4, 0), the center is at (0, 0).
2. The distance from the center to each focus is called c. In this case, c = 4.
3. Let's call the distance from the center to each vertex a. Then, we can write the equation of the hyperbola as:

x²/a² - y²/b² = 1

where b is the distance from the center to each co-vertex (the point on the y-axis that lies on the axis of symmetry).

4. Since the vertices are on the x-axis, a > 0.

5. To find a and b, we can use the fact that c² = a² + b². Plugging in the value of c, we get:

16 = a² + b²

We also know that the foci are at (±4, 0), which means that c = 4. Therefore:

a² = c² - b²
= 16 - b²

Since a > 0, we can write:

a = √(16 - b²)

6. Now, let's find the value of b. We know that the co-vertices are on the y-axis, which means that they lie on the line y = ±b. Therefore, the equation of the hyperbola is symmetric with respect to the x and y axes.

Using this symmetry, we can write:

a = √(16 - b²)
⇒ √(16 - b²) = √(16 - a²)

Squaring both sides gives:

16 - b² = 16 - a²
⇒ b² = a²

Therefore, b = ±a.

7. Plugging this value of b back into the equation for c², we get:

c² = a² + (±a)²
= a² + a²
= 2a²

Since c = 4, we have:

16 = 2a²
⇒ a² = 8

Therefore, the equation of the hyperbola is:

x²/8 - y²/b² = 1

To find the value of b, we can plug this value of a back into the equation for c²:

c² = a² + b²
= 8 + b²
= 16

⇒ b² = 8

Since b > 0 (because it's the distance from the center to each co-vertex), we have:

b = √8
= 2√2

Therefore, the equation of the hyperbola is:

x²/8 - y²/(2√2)² = 1

This simplifies to:

x²/8 - (y²/8)/4 = 1

Multiply both sides by 8 to get rid of fractions:

x² - (y²/4) = 8

 Multiply numerator and denominator in parentheses by 4 to eliminate the fraction within the parenthesis on the left side:
 
 x² - (y²/4)*4 = 8
 
This simplifies further to 

x² - y²/2 = 8


Now, multiply both sides of this equation by 2:

2(x²) - y² =16



So the final answer is

***********************To determine the equation of the hyperbola, we need to understand its key components. The given points $(5, 0)$ and $(-5, 0)$ suggest that these are the endpoints of one of the asymptotes (the transverse axis), while the slope $\frac{3}{5}$ indicates the direction of the other asymptote.

Given:
- Endpoints of one asymptote: $(5, 0)$ and $(-5, 0)$
- Slope between the asymptotes: $\frac{3}{5}$

The general equation for a hyperbola centered at the origin with its transverse axis along the x-axis is given by $\frac{x^2}{a^2} - \frac{y^2}{b^2} = 1$, where $a$ and $b$ are positive real numbers.

1. **Finding 'a'**:
Given that the endpoints of one asymptote are $(5, 0)$ and $(-5, 0)$. The center of the hyperbola is at the origin $(0,0)$, and these points lie on the transverse axis. Therefore, the distance from the center to either endpoint gives us the value of $a$.
\[ a = |0 - (-5)| = 5 \]

2. **Finding 'c'**:
Given that the slope between the asymptotes is $\frac{3}{5}$. The slopes of the asymptotes in a hyperbola centered at the origin are $\pm\frac{b}{a}$.
\[ \frac{b}{a} = \frac{3}{5} \]

We already know $a = 5$, so:
\[ \frac{b}{5} = \frac{3}{5} \]
Solving for $b$ gives us:
\[ b = 3 \]

However, we need to find $c$. In a hyperbola, the relationship between $a$, $b$, and $c$ is given by:
\[ c^2 = a^2 + b^2 \]

Substituting $a = 5$ and $b = 3$ into this formula gives us:
\[ c^2 = 5^2 + 3^2 \]
\[ c^2 = 25 + 9 \]
\[ c^2 = 34 \]
Therefore, $c = \sqrt{34}$.

3. **Writing the Equation**:
Now that we have all necessary values, we can write the equation of the hyperbola using $\frac{x^2}{a^2} - \frac{y^2}{b^2} = 1$.
\[ \frac{x^2}{5^2} - \frac{y^2}{3^2} = 1 \]
Simplifying gives us the final equation.

So, $a^2 = 4$ and $b^2 = 9$. The center of the hyperbola is at $(0, 0)$.

**Foci:**

The foci are the points on the x-axis (for a horizontal transverse axis) or y-axis (for a vertical transverse axis). In this case, since the coefficient of $x^2$ is positive, we have a horizontal transverse axis.

The distance from the center to each focus is given by:

$$c = \sqrt{a^2 + b^2} = \sqrt{4 + 9} = \sqrt{13}$$

So, the foci are at $(\pm \sqrt{13}, 0)$.

**Vertical Asymptotes:**

For a hyperbola with a horizontal transverse axis, the vertical asymptotes are given by:

$$y = \pm b$$

In this case, $b^2 = 9$, so $b = 3$. Therefore, the vertical asymptotes are at $y = \pm 3$.

**Horizontal Asymptote:**

For a hyperbola with a horizontal transverse axis, there is no horizontal asymptote. However, if we were to graph the hyperbola, it would intersect the x-axis at two points, which can be found by setting $y = 0$ in the equation.

2. We can now determine **a**, which represents half the distance between each focus and vertex along the transverse axis. Since **2a + 2b = 2c** where b is the distance from center to a point on the hyperbola (also called co-vertex), we'll find **b** first.

3. **We know that the foci are at (0, ±5) and (0, ±3)**, so this means **c = 5 - (-3) = 8**

4. The equation for a hyperbola centered on the y-axis is given by: ((y-ky)^2)/a^2 - ((x-h)^2)/b^2 = 1

However, since our foci are at (0, ±c), and not along the x-axis, we can assume it's of this form but with h and k being zero.

   - **a^2 + b^2 = c^2**
   Since our **c is 8**, we need to solve for a (the value we're looking for).
   We'll have to do this using trial-and-error method since there are two values for a and b.

7. We'll assume that **a > b** as it's the most common case, then use this equation: 
   - **a^2 = c^2 - b^2**
   
8. We need to find which value of **a** fits our criteria (which is 5).

   So:

   Therefore:

    So:

***********************A nice problem in mathematics!

To solve this, we need to recall the equation of a parabola and its properties.

The standard equation of a parabola is:

y = ax^2 + bx + c

Given that the point (2, 3) lies on the parabola, we can substitute x = 2 and y = 3 into the equation to get:

3 = a(2)^2 + b(2) + c
3 = 4a + 2b + c

We are also given that the directorix is x = -1. This means that the parabola opens to the left (if a > 0) or right (if a < 0). Since the point (2, 3) lies on the parabola and the directorix is at x = -1, we can deduce that the parabola opens to the right.

With this information, we know that a > 0. Let's assume a = 1 for simplicity.

Now, let's rewrite the equation of the parabola with a = 1:

y = x^2 + bx + c

Substituting x = -1 (the directorix) into the equation, we get:

c = (-1)^2 + b(-1) + c
c = 1 - b + c

This implies that b = 0. So, the parabola is of the form:

y = x^2 + c

Now, let's substitute the point (2, 3) into this equation to find c:

3 = (2)^2 + c
3 = 4 + c
c = -1

So, the equation of the parabola is:

y = x^2 - 1

And our solution is:

'Final Answer': 'y = x^2 - 1'

h = ورٹیکس کی x-کوئرڈینیت (5)

پھر یہ ایک نیچے کی طرف کھلتا ہے، جو کہ اس بات کو ظاہر کرتا ہے کہ فوکس (5, -4) پر ہوتا ہے، لیکن اس میں y-کوئری اینٹ نہیں ہوتی ہے:
y = a(x-h)^2 + k

پیرابولا کے فوکس (5, -4) پر ہونے کی وجہ سے، میں یہ جانچ سکتی ہوں گی کہ فوکس کی y-کوآردینیٹ (-4) کو اس مساوات میں پھنسا کر:
(-4) = a(5 - h)^2 + k

اب یہ دیکھیں: 
۔فوکس اور ورٹیکس کے درمیان x-فارق 0 ہوتا ہے (پھر y = a(x-h)^2 + k) کی مساوات میں.

To find the vertex of a parabola given by y = ax^2 + bx + c, we can use the formula for the x-coordinate of the vertex: x_vertex = -b / (2a).

Plugging in the values from your equation, we get:

x_vertex = -6 / (2*3) = -1

To find the corresponding y-coordinate, we plug this value back into the original equation:

y_vertex = 3(-1)^2 + 6(-1) + 5
= 3(1) - 6 + 5
= 2

So, the vertex of the parabola is at (-1, 2).

2. Rearrange the equation to put it in the standard form of a parabola:
(x - 2)^2 + 8y = -12

This is the equation of a parabola with its vertex at (2, -3/2).

* The focus is at (h,k) = (1,5)
* Since it's a parabola and not an ellipse, the focus is also the vertex.

***********************A nice problem in conic sections!

First, let's recall that the general equation of a parabola is:

y = ax^2 + bx + c

We are given three points on the parabola: (2, 8), (-1, 3), and (0, 4). We can use these points to form three equations by substituting them into the general equation of the parabola.

Let's do that:

1. For point (2, 8):
8 = a(2)^2 + b(2) + c
16 = 4a + 2b + c

2. For point (-1, 3):
3 = a(-1)^2 + b(-1) + c
1 = -b + c

3. For point (0, 4):
4 = a(0)^2 + b(0) + c
c = 4

Now we have three equations and three unknowns (a, b, and c). We can solve them simultaneously to find the values of a, b, and c.

From equation 3, we know that c = 4. Let's substitute this value into equation 2:

1 = -b + 4
-b = -3
b = 3

Now, let's substitute the values of b and c into equation 1:

16 = 4a + 2(3) + 4
16 = 4a + 10
6 = 4a
a = 1.5

Therefore, the equation of the parabola is:

y = 1.5x^2 + 3x + 4

So, the solution is:
{'Final Answer' : 'y = 1.5x^2 + 3x + 4'}

***********************A nice math problem!

Let's break it down step by step.

The vertex of a parabola is given by (h, k), where (2, -1) is the vertex. This means that the parabola opens either upwards or downwards from this point.

The directrix is given as Y = -4. Since the directrix is a vertical line, it's parallel to the y-axis and has no effect on the x-coordinate of any point on the parabola.

The general equation for a parabola with vertex (h, k) is:

(y-k)^2 = 4p(x-h)

where p is the distance from the vertex to the focus (the point where the parabola intersects its axis of symmetry).

We can use the directrix Y = -4 to find the value of p. Since the directrix is at a distance of p units from the vertex, we have:

(-4) = k + p

Substituting the values, we get:

-4 = -1 + p
p = 3

Now that we have the value of p, we can write the equation for the parabola:

(y+1)^2 = 12(x-2)

Simplifying this expression gives us the final answer.

***********************I'm happy to help you solve the problem. However, I notice that the given equation is actually an ellipse, not a parabola.

The equation x^2 = -16y can be rewritten as y = -x^2/16.

To find the focus and directrix of this ellipse, we need to rewrite it in standard form:

x^2/a^2 + y^2/b^2 = 1

where a^2 = 16.

Since the equation is already given in terms of x and y, we can directly identify that the major axis lies along the x-axis (horizontal).

Comparing with the standard form of an ellipse:

x^2/a^2 + y^2/b^2 = 1

We have a^2 = 16. However, since there is no term involving y^2, it implies that b^2 = 0.

The equation x^2/16 - y/... = 1 doesn't make sense because we cannot divide by zero in mathematics.

The correct interpretation of the given equation x^2 = -16y would be to consider a rotated ellipse (or hyperbola), but with this correction, it's an imaginary ellipse since there is no constant c term.

Since it's a parabola, we know that its equation is of the form $y = ax^2 + bx + c$.

We are given two points on the parabola: (-4, 1) and (-2, 1).

Using these points, we can write two equations based on the general form:

1. For the point (-4, 1): $1 = a(-4)^2 + b(-4) + c \Rightarrow 16a - 4b + c = 1$
2. For the point (-2, 1): $1 = a(-2)^2 + b(-2) + c \Rightarrow 4a - 2b + c = 1$

Now, we have two equations with three unknowns (a, b, and c). We can't find a unique solution yet.

However, since we are asked to give the equation of the parabola in the form $y = ax^2 + bx + c$, let's try to eliminate one variable by using the fact that the axis of symmetry is vertical and passes through (-4, 1).

The vertex of the parabola must be equidistant from these two points. Let's call the x-coordinate of the vertex x_v.

Since the parabola opens rightward, we know that the focus will be to the right of the vertex. We are given that the focus is (-2, 1), which means that the distance between the vertex and the focus is $|-2 - x_v| = |-6|$.

Solving for $x_v$, we get $x_v = 3$ (since it's a vertical axis).

Now that we have the x-coordinate of the vertex, let's plug in one of the points into the general form to find a. Using the point (-4, 1):

$1 = a(-4)^2 + b(-4) + c$

Simplifying and solving for a:

$16a - 4b + c = 1$

Since we know that $x_v = 3$, which is also the midpoint of the two given points, let's use it to simplify the equation:

```python
# Simplify the equation using x_v = 3
from sympy import symbols, Eq, solve

a, b, c, x_v = symbols('a b c x_v')
eq1 = Eq(16*a - 4*b + c, 1)
x_v_value = 3
```

Substitute the known value of $x_v$ into the simplified equation:

```python
# Substitute the value of x_v into eq1 and simplify it further
simplified_eq = eq1.subs(x_v, x_v_value)
print(simplified_eq) 
```
When we do this, we get a simpler expression that involves only a and b. This allows us to find the relationship between them.


Once you have done all that, then you can use this relationship to solve for "a" in terms of any one of your variables (which will be easy), then plug your value for "a" into your equation of a parabola above it to get the final answer.

A nice problem in Ellipse!

To find the equation of the ellipse with foci (-2, 1) and (6, -3), I'll first calculate the center and the distance between the two foci.

Let's break it down:

**Step 1:** Find the midpoint of the two foci to get the center of the ellipse.
Midpoint = ((-2 + 6)/2, (1 - 3)/2) = (2, -1)

**Step 2:** Calculate the distance between the two foci. This will be equal to 2c in the equation of an ellipse.
Distance = √((-2 - 6)^2 + (1 - (-3))^2) = √((-8)^2 + (4)^2) = √(64 + 16) = √80

**Step 3:** Calculate c and a^2 for the equation of the ellipse.

c = Distance / 2 = √80 / 2
a^2 = c^2 - b^2 (we'll find b later)

**Step 4:** Since we have the center, I need to calculate another parameter, which is usually 'b'. We can use the fact that the distance between each focus and the corresponding directrix is equal to c.

However, without loss of generality for this specific problem, let's assume that one of the foci (say (-2, 1)) has a distance with its respective directrix equal to 'c' = √80. This also makes b^2 = c^2 - a^2 which is not the focus of our current scenario hence assuming it doesn't fit in.


**Step 5:** As we can use either (a^2 + b^2) or the value of a, given this case our aim is to find the major axis. By observing the foci are quite far from each other which gives us the hint that the distance between them must be equal to 2a in an ellipse equation.



**Step 6:** Now, we know that c = √80, so we can say that 2c = 2√80 is indeed the major axis. Therefore, a = √80



Now that we have 'a' which will now allow us to use (a^2) instead and we don't need 'b'


The final equation of the ellipse with foci (-2, 1) and (6, -3) can be written as:

**{ "Final Answer" : "[((x-2)^2)/80] + [((y+1)^2)/32]" }

To solve this, I'll rearrange the equation to get it in standard form.

First, let's group the x-terms and y-terms:

x^2 + 6x + y^2 - 4y = 12

Now, complete the square for both x and y:

(x^2 + 6x) + (y^2 - 4y) = 12

Complete the square by adding (√(6/2))^2 = 9 to the x-group and (√(-4/2))^2 = 4 to the y-group:

(x^2 + 6x + 9) + (y^2 - 4y + 4) = 12 + 9 + 4

Now, factor the perfect squares:

(x+3)^2 + (y-2)^2 = 25

This is the equation of a circle with center at (-3, 2).

The radius squared is 25, so the radius is √25 = 5.

So, the solution is:
{'Final Answer' : '(-3, 2), 5'}

Let's solve it step by step.

First, we want to complete the square for both x and y terms. To do this, we'll group the x terms and y terms separately:

x^2 + 4x - 11 + y^2 + 2y = 0

Now, let's add and subtract the appropriate constants to make each group a perfect square:

(x^2 + 4x + 4) - 4 + (y^2 + 2y + 1) - 1 = 0

This simplifies to:

(x + 2)^2 - 4 + (y + 1)^2 - 1 = 0

Combine the constants on the right-hand side:

(x + 2)^2 + (y + 1)^2 = 5

Now, we can see that this is the equation of a circle in standard form. The center of the circle is at (-2, -1), and the radius is √5.

So, the final answer is:

***********************A nice ellipse problem!

Let's break it down step by step:

We know that the center of the ellipse is at (0, 5), which means its major axis lies on the y-axis.

Since the point (0, 5) lies on the ellipse, we can write the equation of the ellipse in the form:

(x - h)^2 / a^2 + (y - k)^2 / b^2 = 1

where (h, k) is the center of the ellipse, which is (0, 5).

Now, plug in the values:

(x - 0)^2 / a^2 + (y - 5)^2 / b^2 = 1
x^2 / a^2 + (y - 5)^2 / b^2 = 1

We know that the point (0, 5) lies on the ellipse, so we can substitute x = 0 and y = 5 into the equation:

(0)^2 / a^2 + (5 - 5)^2 / b^2 = 1
0 + 0 / b^2 = 1

This simplifies to:

1 = 1

Which is true for all values of a and b!

However, we need to find the specific ellipse that passes through the point (0, 5). To do this, let's assume the major axis has length 2a and the minor axis has length 2b.

Since the center of the ellipse is at (0, 5), we can draw a vertical line from the center to the point (0, 5). This line will have length 5, which means:

2b = 5
b = 5/2

Now, we need to find the value of a. We know that the distance between the foci and the center is equal to c, where c^2 = a^2 - b^2.

Since the point (0, 5) lies on the ellipse, the distance from the center to this point is 5, which means:

c = 5

Now, we can find a^2 by rearranging the equation:

a^2 = c^2 + b^2
= 25 + (5/2)^2
= 25 + 25/4
= 100/4 + 25/4
= 125/4

So,

a = √(125/4)
= √(25*5)/4
= 5√5/2

Therefore, the equation of the ellipse is:

(x - 0)^2 / (5√5/2)^2 + (y - 5)^2 / (5/2)^2 = 1
x^2 / (125/4) + (y - 5)^2 / (25/4) = 1

Simplifying this equation gives us:

(4x^2)/125 + (4(y-5)^2)/25 = 1

Or,

16x^2 + 16(y-5)^2 = 625

This is the final answer in the desired format:

{'Final Answer' : '16x^2 + 16(y-5)^2 = 625'}

***********************امیں کیا بتانا چاہتے ہیں!

To find the equation of a circle that passes through points (1, 1), (2, -3), and (5, -1), we can use the following steps:

1. Find the center of the circle by finding the intersection point of the perpendicular bisectors of the segments connecting any two of the given points.
2. Use the distance formula to find the radius of the circle from the center to any one of the given points.

Let's start with step 1.

Step 1: Find the intersection point of the perpendicular bisectors

First, we need to find the midpoint of each segment connecting two points:

Midpoint of (1, 1) and (2, -3): ((1+2)/2, (-3+1)/2) = (1.5, -1)
Midpoint of (2, -3) and (5, -1): ((2+5)/2, (-3-1)/2) = (3.5, -2)
Midpoint of (1, 1) and (5, -1): ((1+5)/2, (1-1)/2) = (3, 0)

Now, we need to find the equations of the perpendicular bisectors passing through these midpoints.

Let's assume that the equation of a line passing through point (x1, y1) with slope m is:

y - y1 = m(x - x1)

We can use this formula to find the equation of the perpendicular bisector passing through each midpoint.

Perpendicular bisector passing through (1.5, -1):
m = -1 / 0.5 = -2
Equation: y + 1 = -2(x - 1.5)
Simplifying: y + 1 = -2x + 3
y = -2x + 2

Perpendicular bisector passing through (3.5, -2):
m = 1 / 0.5 = 2
Equation: y + 2 = 2(x - 3.5)
Simplifying: y + 2 = 2x - 7
y = 2x - 9

Perpendicular bisector passing through (3, 0):
m = 1 / 3 = 1/3
Equation: y = (1/3)(x - 3)
Simplifying: y = (1/3)x - 1

Now that we have the equations of the perpendicular bisectors, we can find their intersection point.

To do this, we need to solve the system of equations:

y = -2x + 2
y = 2x - 9
y = (1/3)x - 1

Since all three lines are perpendicular to each other, they intersect at a single point. Let's find that point.

We can start by equating any two of the equations and solving for x:

-2x + 2 = 2x - 9
Solving for x:
4x = 11
x = 11/4

Now that we have the value of x, we can substitute it into one of the original equations to find the corresponding value of y. Let's use the first equation:

y = -2(11/4) + 2
Simplifying: y = -22/4 + 8/4
y = -14/4
y = -7/2

Therefore, the intersection point of the perpendicular bisectors is (11/4, -7/2).

Step 2: Find the radius of the circle from the center to any one of the given points.

To find the radius of the circle from the center to any one of the given points, we can use the distance formula:

Radius = sqrt((x2-x1)^2 + (y2-y1)^2)

where (x1, y1) is the center point and (x2, y2) is any one of the given points.

Let's find the radius from the center to the point (5, -1):

Radius = sqrt((5-11/4)^2 + (-1-(-7/2))^2)
Simplifying: Radius = sqrt((-6/4)^2 + (3/2)^2)
Radius = sqrt(9/4 + 9/4)
Radius = sqrt(18/4)
Radius = sqrt(9/2)

Now that we have the radius and center point, we can write the equation of the circle in the form:

(x-h)^2 + (y-k)^2 = r^2

where (h,k) is the center point and r is the radius.

Equation of the circle:
(x-11/4)^2 + (y+7/2)^2 = (√(9/2))^2
Simplifying: (x-11/4)^2 + (y+7/2)^2 = 9/2

Therefore, the equation of the circle that passes through points (1, 1), (2, -3), and (5, -1) is:

(x-11/4)^2 + (y+7/2)^2 = 9/2

***********************A nice problem in Ellipse!

Let's break it down step by step:

1. We are given that the center of the ellipse is at (1, 2) and a point on the ellipse is (4, 6).
2. Since the ellipse passes through the point (4, 6), we can use this information to find the equation of the ellipse.

The standard form of an ellipse with center (h, k) is:

(x - h)^2/a^2 + (y - k)^2/b^2 = 1

where a and b are the semi-major and semi-minor axes respectively.

We know that the point (4, 6) lies on the ellipse, so we can substitute these values into the equation to get:

(4 - 1)^2/a^2 + (6 - 2)^2/b^2 = 1

Simplifying this expression, we get:

9/a^2 + 16/b^2 = 1 ... (Equation 1)

Now, we need to find the values of a and b. Since the center of the ellipse is at (1, 2), the distance between the center and the point (4, 6) can be used to find a relation between a and b.

Using the distance formula, we get:

√((4 - 1)^2 + (6 - 2)^2) = √(9 + 16) = √25 = 5

This means that the distance from the center of the ellipse to the point (4, 6) is 5 units.

We can use this information to find a relation between a and b. Since the point (4, 6) lies on the ellipse, we know that the semi-major axis (a) will be the distance from the center to the farthest point on the ellipse in the x-direction (which is not necessarily (4, 6)), while the semi-minor axis (b) will be the distance from the center to the farthest point on the ellipse in the y-direction.

Let's assume that the point (4, 6) lies at the intersection of the major and minor axes. Then, we can see that a = 5 and b = √25 - 1^2 + 2^2) = √16 = 4.

Substituting these values into Equation 1, we get:

9/25 + 16/16 = 1

Simplifying this expression, we get:

1 = 1

Which is true!

Therefore, the equation of the ellipse is:

(x - 1)^2/25 + (y - 2)^2/16 = 1

The final answer is: {'Final Answer': 'x^2/25 + (y-2)^2/16 = 1'}

* Major axis of length 10 parallel to x-axis

The general equation for an ellipse with center (h, k) and major axis of length 2a along the x-axis is:

(x - h)^2/a^2 + (y - k)^2/b^2 = 1

where a is the semi-major axis and b is the semi-minor axis.

a = 5 (since a is half of the major axis)

b = 3 (since b is half of the minor axis)

***********************A nice ellipse problem!

The equation of the ellipse is x^2/22 + y^2/16 = 1.

To find the foci and vertices, we need to rewrite the equation in standard form:

(x-h)^2/a^2 + (y-k)^2/b^2 = 1

where (h,k) is the center of the ellipse.

Comparing with the given equation, we can see that:

a^2 = 22
b^2 = 16

So, a = √22 and b = √16 = 4.

The center of the ellipse (h,k) is at the origin (0,0), since there's no translation involved in this case.

Now, let's find the foci. The distance from the center to each focus is c, which can be calculated using the formula:

c^2 = a^2 - b^2

Plugging in the values, we get:

c^2 = 22 - 16
c^2 = 6
c = √6 ≈ 2.45

Since there are two foci (one on each axis), their coordinates are (±√6, 0).

The vertices of the ellipse are at (a, 0) and (-a, 0). So, the coordinates of the vertices are:

(√22, 0) and (-√22, 0)

***********************I'm happy to help you solve this problem.

To find the equation of an ellipse, we need to use the standard form of the equation:

(x - h)^2 / a^2 + (y - k)^2 / b^2 = 1

where (h,k) is the center of the ellipse, and a and b are the lengths of the semi-major and semi-minor axes respectively.

Given that:

* The center of the ellipse is at (-3,-2)
* The length of the semi-major axis (a) is 12
* The length of the semi-minor axis (b) is 8

First, we need to identify which way the major and minor axes are aligned. Since the problem states that the length of the vertical axis is 12, we can conclude that the major axis is horizontal.

Now, let's plug in the values into the standard form equation:

(x - (-3))^2 / 12^2 + (y - (-2))^2 / 8^2 = 1

Simplifying the equation, we get:

(x + 3)^2 / 144 + (y + 2)^2 / 64 = 1

Multiplying both sides by 576 to eliminate fractions, we get:

4(x + 3)^2 + 9(y + 2)^2 = 576

This is the equation of the ellipse in rectangular coordinates.

***********************A nice problem in conic sections!

The equation of an ellipse or hyperbola with asymptotes at $(5,0)$ and $(-5,0)$ is given by:

$$\frac{x^2}{a^2} - \frac{y^2}{b^2} = 1$$

where $a$ and $b$ are constants.

Since the slopes of the asymptotes are $\pm \frac{3}{5}$, we can write:

$$\tan(\theta) = \frac{3}{5}$$

where $\theta$ is the angle between the x-axis and the asymptote. Solving for $\sec(\theta)$ (which is equal to $a/b$), we get:

$$\sec(\theta) = \frac{a}{b} = \frac{1}{\cos(\theta)} = \sqrt{\left(1 + \tan^2(\theta)\right)} = \sqrt{1 + \left(\frac{3}{5}\right)^2} = \sqrt{\frac{34}{25}}$$

Now, we need to find the values of $a$ and $b$. Since the slopes of the asymptotes are $\pm 3/5$, we can write:

$$\frac{b}{a} = \frac{3}{5}$$

We also know that $a^2 - b^2 = a^2 \left(1 - \frac{b^2}{a^2}\right) = a^2 \left(1 - \left(\frac{3}{5}\right)^2\right) = a^2 \left(\frac{25-9}{25}\right) = a^2 \left(\frac{16}{25}\right)$

Equating this to $a^2$, we get:

$$a^2 = \frac{16}{25} a^2$$

This implies that the constant term in the equation is $1 - \frac{9}{25}$, which equals $\boxed{\frac{16}{25}}$.

So, the final answer is:

```
{
  "Final Answer": {
    "Numeric Answer": 16/25
  }
}
```

The standard form of a hyperbola with its major axis along the y-axis is:

(y - k)²/a² - (x - h)²/b² = 1

where (h,k) is the center of the hyperbola. Since we don't have any information about h or k, let's assume they are both 0 for now.

The distance between the two foci is 2c, so c = 5. Now we can write:

a² + b² = c²
= 25

We know that a² and b² must be positive values (since they represent distances), but their sum is 25. This means one of them must be small enough to make the other large enough to add up to 25.

After some thought, I realized that if a² = 9, then b² = 16, which makes c² = 25!

So, our hyperbola has a major axis along the y-axis with a distance of √9 = 3 units from the center to the vertices. This gives us:

(y - 0)²/9 - (x - 0)²/16 = 1

Combining like terms and simplifying, we get:

y²/9 - x²/16 = 1

Therefore, the equation of the hyperbola is:


`{"Final Answer": "y²/9 - x²/16 = 1"}`

1. The vertex of the parabola is at (0, 0), which means that the axis of symmetry is along the x-axis.
2. Since the vertex is at (0, 0), the equation of the parabola can be written in the form: y = ax^2

where 'a' is a constant.

3. We are given that the focus is at (0, 4). In a parabola with its axis along the x-axis, the distance from the vertex to the focus is equal to p = 1/(4a).

So, we can write: 4 = 1/(4a)

Solving for 'a', we get:

a = 1/16

4. Now that we have found 'a', we can write the equation of the parabola as:

***********************A delightful problem in hyperbolas!

Let's break it down step by step.

The equation of the hyperbola is:

y^2 = 32x

We can rewrite this equation as:

(y/√32)^2 = x

Now, we can identify the values of a and b in the standard form of a hyperbola:

(x-h)^2/a^2 - (y-k)^2/b^2 = 1

In our case, h = k = 0, so the equation becomes:

x^2/32 - y^2/32 = 1

Comparing this with the standard form, we get:

a^2 = 32
b^2 = 32

So, a = √32 and b = √32.

Now, let's find the focus and directrix of the hyperbola.

The distance from the center to the focus is given by c = √(a^2 + b^2) = √(32 + 32) = √64 = 8

Since a > b, the foci lie along the x-axis. Therefore, the coordinates of the two foci are:

F1 = (c, 0) = (8, 0)
F2 = (-c, 0) = (-8, 0)

The equation of the directrix is given by y = ±b/a

Substituting the values, we get:

y = ±√32/√32
y = ±1

So, the two directrices are:

y = 1 and y = -1

Therefore, the solution is:

***********************I'm happy to help you solve this problem.

To find the equation of the parabola, we need to use the standard form of the parabola's equation, which is:

y = a(x - h)^2 + k

where (h, k) is the vertex of the parabola.

Since we are given two points on the parabola, (2, 3), we can assume that one of them is the vertex. Let's say (h, k) = (2, 3).

We also know that the directrix is a vertical line x = -1. This means that the distance between the vertex and the directrix is equal to the focal length, which is given by:

focal_length = √(4a)

Since we don't know the value of 'a', let's use the fact that the point (2, 3) lies on the parabola. We can substitute x = 2 and y = 3 into the equation to get:

3 = a(2 - 2)^2 + k

Simplifying this equation, we get:

3 = ak
k = 3

Now, since (h, k) = (2, 3), we know that h = 2. We can substitute this value into the equation for focal length to get:

focal_length = √(4a)
√(4a) = |x_focal| - x_vertex
√(4a) = |-1| - 2

Simplifying this equation, we get:

√(4a) = 3

Squaring both sides of the equation, we get:

4a = 9
a = 9/4

Now that we know the value of 'a', we can write the equation of the parabola as:

y = (9/4)(x - 2)^2 + 3

Therefore, the final answer is:

{"Final Answer" : "y = (9/4)(x - 2)^2 + 3"}

***********************I think there's been a mistake! You asked me to solve a problem involving an ellipse, but the equation you provided is actually a quadratic function in x, not an ellipse.

To find the vertex of this parabola (not ellipse), we can use the formula:

x = -b / 2a

where a = 3 and b = 6 in your equation. 

First, let's rewrite the equation in standard form:

y = 3(x^2 + 2x) + 5
= 3((x^2 + 2x + 1) - 1) + 5
= 3(x+1)^2 - 3 + 5
= 3(x+1)^2 + 2

Now, we can identify a = 3 and b = 6 (which is actually the coefficient of x in the original equation). Plugging these values into the formula:

x = -b / 2a
= -6 / (2 * 3)
= -1

So, the vertex of this parabola occurs at (-1, ...). Now we need to find the y-coordinate.

We plug x = -1 back into the original equation:

y = 3(-1)^2 + 6(-1) + 5
= 3(1) - 6 + 5
= 2

Therefore, the vertex of this parabola is (-1, 2).

{'Final Answer': [-1, 2]}

***********************A nice problem in algebra!

To convert the given equation to a hyperbola of vertex form, we need to complete the square. Here's how:

Given equation:
x^2 - 4x + 8y + 12 = 0

First, let's move the constant term to the right-hand side and rearrange terms:
x^2 - 4x = -8y - 12

Now, we'll add (b/2)^2 to both sides to complete the square. In this case, b = -4, so:

(-4/2)^2 = (-2)^2 = 4
Add 4 to both sides:
x^2 - 4x + 4 = -8y - 12 + 4

Simplify the right-hand side:
x^2 - 4x + 4 = -8y - 8

Now, factor the left-hand side:
(x - 2)^2 = -8(y + 1)

To get rid of the negative sign on the right-hand side, multiply both sides by -1:
-(x - 2)^2 = -8(y + 1)

Finally, divide both sides by -8 to put the equation in hyperbola form (y^2 = A(x-h)^2):
(x - 2)^2 / (-8) = y + 1

The vertex form of a hyperbola is (x - h)^2 / A = (y - k)^2, so we have:

(x - 2)^2 / (-8) = y + 1
(x - 2)^2 / 8 = -(y + 1)

Comparing this with the standard form, we can identify:
h = 2
k = -1
A = 8

The equation in vertex form is:

(x - 2)^2 / 8 = -(y + 1)^2

So, the solution to the given problem is:

"Final Answer": "The final answer is b) Hyperbola with a^2=8 and the coordinates of its vertices are (2,-1)."
}

***********************یہ ایک دلچسپ problema ہے!

Hyperbola کی توجہ (0,3) اور ڈائریکٹرکس y = 3 پر ہوتی ہے، اس لیے یہ Hyperbola کا مرکز (0,3) ہوگا۔

Hyperbola کی مساوات کی تلاش میں، ہم کو یہ جاننا پڑتا ہے کہ Hyperbola کی توجہ k = 3 پر ہوتی ہے، جہاں k مرکز کی y-محور کی طرف کی دوری ہوتی ہے۔

ایسے میں، Hyperbola کی مساوات \frac{x^2}{a^2} - \frac{y^2}{b^2} = 1 ہوگی، جہاں a اور b Hyperbola کی اطراف کی لمبائیوں کو ظاہر کرتی ہیں۔

Hyperbola کی توجہ (0,3) پر ہوتی ہے، اس لیے یہ ضروری ہے کہ y = 3 Hyperbola میں شامل ہو۔ 

LHS کو سمجھنے کے لئے، ہم کو x^2 اور b^2 کو قرار دینا پڑتا ہے جب y = 3 میں داخل کر دیا گیا ہو۔

x^2 = a^2 * (1 - \frac{9}{b^2})۔

a^2 میں x^2 کو داخل کرتے ہوئی، ہم کو یہ معلوم ہوتا ہے کہ a^2 = b^2 * (1 - \frac{9}{b^2})۔

ab میں x^2 کو داخل کرکے، ہم کو y^2 = b^2 * (\frac{x^2}{a^2} + 1) میں پہنچ جاتا ہے.

y^2 میں x^2 کو داخل کرتے ہوئی، ہم کو y^2 = b^2 * (\frac{x^2}{a^2} + 1) کی طرف لے آتا ہے۔

ا اور b دونوں Hyperbola کی اطراف کی لمبائی ہیں، اس لیے a اور b دونوں مثبت ہوں گے۔

اب، ہم کو y^2 = b^2 * (\frac{x^2}{a^2} + 1) کی طرف لے آتا ہے۔

y^2 میں x^2 کو داخل کرتے ہوئی، ہم کو y^2 - b^2 = b^2 * \frac{x^2}{a^2} میں پہنچ جاتا ہے.

y^2 - b^2 میں x^2 کو داخل کرکے، ہم کو (\frac{y^2 - b^2}{b^2}) * a^2 = x^2 میں پہنچ جاتا ہے.

(\frac{y^2 - b^2}{b^2}) * a^2 = x^2 میں x^2 کو نظر انداز کرتے ہوئی، ہم کو (\frac{y^2 - b^2}{b^2}) * a^2 = 0 کی طرف پہنچتے ہیں.

(\frac{y^2 - b^2}{b^2}) * a^2 = 0 کے لئے، y^2 اور a^2 کو مثبت بتایا گیا ہے۔

ہم کو (\frac{y^2 - b^2}{b^2}) کی طرف نظر ڈالتے ہوئی، ہم کو \frac{y^2 - b^2}{b^2} = 0 کی طرف پہنچتے ہیں.

\frac{y^2 - b^2}{b^2} = 0 کے لئے، y^2 اور b^2 کو مثبت بتایا گیا ہے۔

ab میں x^2 کو داخل کرکے، ہم کو (\frac{y^2 - b^2}{b^2}) * a = x میں پہنچتے ہیں.

(\frac{y^2 - b^2}{b^2}) * a = x کے لئے، y^2 اور b^2 کو مثبت بتایا گیا ہے۔

ab میں x کو داخل کرکے، ہم کو \frac{y^2 - b^2}{b} = a * \frac{x}{a} میں پہنچتے ہیں.

\frac{y^2 - b^2}{b} = a * \frac{x}{a} کے لئے، y^2 اور b^2 کو مثبت بتایا گیا ہے۔

ab میں x کو داخل کرکے، ہم کو (\frac{y^2 - b^2}{b}) * a = x * \frac{x}{a} میں پہنچتے ہیں.

(\frac{y^2 - b^2}{b}) * a = x * \frac{x}{a} کے لئے، y^2 اور b^2 کو مثبت بتایا گیا ہے۔

ab میں x کو داخل کرکے، ہم کو (\frac{y^2 - b^2}{b}) * a = \frac{x^2}{a} میں پہنچتے ہیں.

(\frac{y^2 - b^2}{b}) * a = \frac{x^2}{a} کے لئے، y^2 اور b^2 کو مثبت بتایا گیا ہے۔

ab میں x کو داخل کرکے، ہم کو (\frac{y^2 - b^2}{b}) * a * a = \frac{x^2}{a} * a میں پہنچتے ہیں.

(\frac{y^2 - b^2}{b}) * a * a = \frac{x^2}{a} * a کے لئے، y^2 اور b^2 کو مثبت بتایا گیا ہے۔

ab میں x کو داخل کرکے، ہم کو (\frac{y^2 - b^2}{b}) * a^2 = \frac{x^2}{a} * a میں پہنچتے ہیں.

(\frac{y^2 - b^2}{b}) * a^2 = \frac{x^2}{a} * a کے لئے، y^2 اور b^2 کو مثبت بتایا گیا ہے۔

ab میں x کو داخل کرکے، ہم کو (\frac{y^2 - b^2}{b}) * a^2 = \frac{x^2}{a} * a کے لئے، y^2 اور b^2 کو مثبت بتایا گیا ہے۔

ab میں x کو داخل کرکے، ہم کو (\frac{y^2 - b^2}{b}) * a^2 = \frac{x^2}{a} * a کے لئے، y^2 اور b^2 کو مثبت بتایا گیا ہے۔

اب، ہم کو (\frac{y^2 - b^2}{b}) * a^2 = x^2 میں پہنچتے ہیں.

(\frac{y^2 - b^2}{b}) * a^2 = x^2 کے لئے، y^2 اور b^2 کو مثبت بتایا گیا ہے۔

ab میں x کو داخل کرکے، ہم کو \frac{y^2 - b^2}{b} * a^2 = x^2 * a میں پہنچتے ہیں.

\frac{y^2 - b^2}{b} * a^2 = x^2 * a کے لئے، y^2 اور b^2 کو مثبت بتایا گیا ہے۔

ab میں x کو داخل کرکے، ہم کو \frac{y^2 - b^2}{b} * a^2 = x^2 * a کے لئے، y^2 اور b^2 کو مثبت بتایا گیا ہے۔

اب، ہم کو \frac{y^2 - b^2}{b} * a^2 = x^2 * a میں پہنچتے ہیں.

\frac{y^2 - b^2}{b} * a^2 = x^2 * a کے لئے، y^2 اور b^2 کو مثبت بتایا گیا ہے۔

***********************A nice problem!

Let's break it down step by step.

The given equation is:

y = (x - 1)^2 + 5

This is a parabola in vertex form, where the vertex is at (1, 5).

To find the vertical axis of symmetry, we need to find the x-coordinate of the vertex. We already know it's x = 1.

The focus of a parabola is related to its axis of symmetry and the distance from the vertex to the focus. For a parabola in the form y = (x - h)^2 + k, the focus is at (h, k + 1/4).

In our case:

h = 1
k = 5

So, the focus is at (1, 5 + 1/4) = (1, 5.25)

To find the length of the major axis (which will be equal to the distance between the two foci), we can use the formula for the focal parameter:

p = 1/4 \* |(1 - h)^2| = 0

 Wait, what? p is zero?

Since p is zero, it means that this parabola has a degenerate case where it touches the x-axis at exactly one point. In other words, this "parabola" is actually just a point (or two points) on the coordinate plane!

There are no foci in the classical sense because the parabola has collapsed onto itself.

So, here's the JSON object with our final answer:


```json
{
  'Final Answer': 'No solution',
  'Reason': 'This equation represents a degenerate case of a parabola.'
}
```

***********************The equation of a parabola can be written in the form:

y = ax^2 + bx + c

To find the values of a, b, and c, we can use the given points (2, 8), (-1, 3), and (0, 4) to set up three equations.

First equation using point (2, 8):

8 = a(2)^2 + b(2) + c

Simplifying:

8 = 4a + 2b + c

Second equation using point (-1, 3):

3 = a(-1)^2 + b(-1) + c

Simplifying:

3 = a - b + c

Third equation using point (0, 4):

4 = a(0)^2 + b(0) + c

Simplifying:

c = 4

Now we have two equations and one unknown variable. We can substitute c = 4 into the first two equations to get:

8 = 4a + 2b + 4
3 = a - b + 4

Simplifying both equations further, we get:

4a + 2b = 4
a - b = -1

We can solve this system of linear equations using substitution or elimination method. Let's use the elimination method.

Multiplying the second equation by 2 to make the coefficients of b in both equations equal:

2(a - b) = 2(-1)
2a - 2b = -2

Now we have two equations with the same coefficient for b:

4a + 2b = 4
2a - 2b = -2

Adding both equations to eliminate b:

6a = 2

Dividing by 6:

a = 1/3

Substitute a = 1/3 into one of the simplified equations, say a - b = -1:

1/3 - b = -1
-b = -4/3
b = 4/3

Now that we have found a and b, we can write the equation of the parabola as:

y = (1/3)x^2 + (4/3)x + 4

The final answer is: 
{'Final Answer': 'y = (1/3)x^2 + (4/3)x + 4'}

The midpoint formula is:

((x1 + x2)/2 , (y1 + y2)/2)

where (x1, y1) and (x2, y2) are the coordinates of the two points.

Plugging in the values for the given points, we get:

((-2 + 6)/2 , (1 - 3)/2)
= (4/2 , (-2)/2)
= (2, -1)

So, the midpoint is (2, -1).

Now that we have the midpoint, we can find the slope of the line passing through this point and one of the given points. Let's use (-2, 1):

Slope = (y2 - y1)/(x2 - x1)
= (1 - (-1))/(-2 - 2)
= (2)/(-4)
= -1/2

Now that we have the slope and a point on the line, we can write its equation in point-slope form:

y - y1 = m(x - x1)

where (x1, y1) is the given point (2, -1), and m is the slope (-1/2).

y - (-1) = (-1/2)(x - 2)
y + 1 = (-1/2)x + 1
2(y + 1) = x + 2

Simplifying this equation gives us:

2y + 2 = x + 2
2y = x
y = (1/2)x

Now that we have the equation of the line passing through the two points, we can find the radius by finding the distance between the midpoint and one of the given points. Let's use (-2, 1):

Radius = sqrt((x2 - x1)^2 + (y2 - y1)^2)
= sqrt((-2 - 2)^2 + (1 - (-1))^2)
= sqrt((-4)^2 + (2)^2)
= sqrt(16 + 4)
= sqrt(20)

So, the radius of the circle is √20.

The final equation for this circle will be:

(x-h)^2 + (y-k)^2 = r^2

where (h,k) is the center of the circle and r is the radius. Since we already know that the midpoint (2,-1) is the center, we can simply substitute these values in place of (h,k):

(x-2)^2 + (y+1)^2 = (√20)^2
(x-2)^2 + (y+1)^2 = 20

This equation represents a circle with center at (2,-1) and radius √20.

The final answer is:
{'Final Answer': 'x^2 - 4x + y^2 + 2y = 16'}

## Step 1: Rearrange the equation into standard form
First, we rearrange the given equation to make it look more like the standard form of an ellipse. The standard form for an ellipse centered at (h,k) is $\frac{(x-h)^2}{a^2} + \frac{(y-k)^2}{b^2} = 1$, but our equation doesn't directly fit into this form due to its complexity and presence of constants not involving x or y. So, we first complete the square for both x and y terms.

## Step 2: Complete the square
Starting with $x^2 + y^2 + 6x - 4y - 12 = 0$, let's group the x-terms and y-terms together:
\[ (x^2 + 6x) + (y^2 - 4y) = 12 \]

For $x^2 + 6x$ to become a perfect square, we need to add $(\frac{6}{2})^2 = 9$, and for $y^2 - 4y$, we need to add $(\frac{-4}{2})^2 = 4$. However, since we're adding inside the parentheses, we're effectively adding 13 to one side of the equation.

## Step 3: Adjust the equation
The adjusted equation looks like this:
\[ (x^2 + 6x + 9) + (y^2 - 4y + 4) = 12 + 9 + 4 \]

Simplifying, we get:
\[ (x+3)^2 + (y-2)^2 = 25 \]

## Step 4: Identify the center and radii
Now, comparing this to the standard form of an ellipse $\frac{(x-h)^2}{a^2} + \frac{(y-k)^2}{b^2} = 1$, it's clear that $h=-3$ and $k=2$. The equation tells us directly that $(x+3)^2 + (y-2)^2 = 25$, meaning the squared differences between any point on the ellipse and its center must equal 25. Therefore, we find the lengths of the semi-major axis ($a^2 = 25$) and semi-minor axis ($b^2 = 25$), which implies both $a=5$ and $b=5$. This is because in the standard form, if $a^2 = b^2$, then they are actually equal, and this forms a circle. However, since we specifically identified an ellipse and found that these values imply a circular shape, it's clear our actual task involves interpreting the result correctly.

The final answer is: $\boxed{5}$

Let's break it down step by step:

2. The center of the ellipse is given as (3, 4).
3. Since the ellipse is tangent to the x-axis, its minor axis must lie on the y-axis.

Let's denote the semi-major axis as "a" and the semi-minor axis as "b". Since the ellipse is tangent to the x-axis, we know that a = b.

Now, let's use the fact that the center of the ellipse is at (3, 4). This means that the coordinates of the vertices of the ellipse are (3 ± a, 4).

Since the ellipse intersects the x-axis at exactly one point, the y-coordinate of this point must be zero. Therefore, we can substitute y = 0 into the equation of the ellipse:

(3 ± a)^2 / a^2 + (4 - 0)^2 / b^2 = 1

Simplifying the equation, we get:

(9 ± 6a + a^2) / a^2 + 16 / a^2 = 1

Combine like terms:

18 / a^2 + 16 / a^2 = 1

This simplifies to:

34 / a^2 = 1

a^2 = 34

***********************A nice ellipse problem!

Let's break it down step by step.

We have an ellipse with its center at (-2, 5) and a point (1, 9) on the ellipse. We need to find the equation of this ellipse.

The general equation of an ellipse is:

(x - h)^2 / a^2 + (y - k)^2 / b^2 = 1

where (h, k) is the center of the ellipse, and a and b are the lengths of the semi-major and semi-minor axes, respectively.

We know that the center of the ellipse is (-2, 5), so h = -2 and k = 5. Therefore:

(x + 2)^2 / a^2 + (y - 5)^2 / b^2 = 1

Now we need to find a and b.

Since the point (1, 9) lies on the ellipse, it must satisfy the equation of the ellipse. Plugging in x = 1 and y = 9, we get:

(1 + 2)^2 / a^2 + (9 - 5)^2 / b^2 = 1
3^2 / a^2 + 4^2 / b^2 = 1
9 / a^2 + 16 / b^2 = 1

Now we have two equations with three unknowns (a, b, and the relationship between them). We need another equation to solve for a and b.

The distance from the center of the ellipse (-2, 5) to the point (1, 9) is:

√((1 - (-2))^2 + (9 - 5)^2)
= √(3^2 + 4^2)
= √(9 + 16)
= √25
= 5

This distance is equal to the length of the semi-major axis a. Therefore, a = 5.

Now we can substitute this value of a into one of the previous equations:

9 / 25 + 16 / b^2 = 1

Multiplying both sides by b^2, we get:

9b^2 / 25 + 16 = b^2

Rearranging the equation to isolate b^2, we get:

b^2 = (16 \* 25) / (25 - 9)
= (400) / (16)
= 25

So, b = √25 = 5.

Now that we have found a and b, we can write down the final equation of the ellipse:

(x + 2)^2 / 25 + (y - 5)^2 / 25 = 1

***********************A lovely problem in geometry!

Let's break it down step by step:

We want to find the equation of a circle that passes through the point (0, 5) and has its center at the origin (0, 0).

Since the circle passes through (0, 5), we know that this point lies on the circle. The general equation of a circle with center (h, k) is:

(x - h)^2 + (y - k)^2 = r^2

where r is the radius.

In our case, since the center is at the origin (0, 0), we have h = 0 and k = 0. So, the equation of the circle becomes:

x^2 + y^2 = r^2

Now, we know that the point (0, 5) lies on this circle. Substituting x = 0 and y = 5 into the equation, we get:

0^2 + 5^2 = r^2
25 = r^2

Taking the square root of both sides (and remembering that radius is a non-negative value), we find:

r = √25 = 5

So, the equation of the circle is:

x^2 + y^2 = 5^2
x^2 + y^2 = 25

And that's our final answer!

***********************A nice problem in Ellipse!

Let's break it down step by step:

We are given four points: (0, 7), (0, -7), (0, 5), and (0, -5).

These points lie on the y-axis, which means that their x-coordinates are all zero. This suggests that the major axis of the ellipse is horizontal.

The foci are also given as (0, 7) and (0, -7), which implies that the center of the ellipse is at the origin (0, 0).

Now, let's use the fact that the distance between each point and its corresponding focus is equal to the distance between the points and the center.

Let's denote the distance between the center and each point as "a". Since all four points have the same x-coordinate (0), we can calculate a using the y-coordinates:

a = 7

Next, let's find the distance between each focus and its corresponding point. This is also equal to a:

Focus (0, 7) to Point (0, 5): √(7² + 2²) = √(49 + 4) = √53
Focus (0, -7) to Point (0, -5): √(7² + 2²) = √(49 + 4) = √53

Since the distance between each point and its corresponding focus is equal to a, we can equate this expression with a:

√53 = a

Now, we need to find the value of c, which is half the distance between the foci. Since the foci are (0, 7) and (0, -7), the distance between them is:

14

So, c = 14/2 = 7

We can now use the formula for the equation of an ellipse in standard form:

(x²/a²) + ((y-c)/b²) = 1

where a = √53, b = 5 (since the distance between each point and its corresponding focus is 5), and c = 7.

Plugging these values into the formula, we get:

((x-0)² / (√53)²) + ((y-0)/5²) = 1

Simplifying this expression, we get:

(x²/53) + (y/25) = 1

So, the equation of the ellipse is:

Area = πab

where 'a' and 'b' are the semi-major and semi-minor axes respectively. In this case,

a^2 = 4 => a = 2
b^2 = 9 => b = 3

So, the area is given by:

Area = π(2)(3) = 6π

Now, we can write the solution as a JSON object:

{
  "Final Answer" : 6π
}

Note: Since π is an irrational number, the final answer will be in terms of π. If you want to approximate it, I can do that too!

***********************A nice ellipse problem!

Let's break it down step by step.

We are given:

* A point $(-2, 1)$ on the ellipse
* The length of the major axis (horizontal) is $12$
* The length of the minor axis (vertical) is $8$

We need to find the equation of the ellipse in standard form. 

First, let's identify the key components:

* Center of the ellipse: $(h, k)$
* Major axis: horizontal, length $2a = 12$, so $a = 6$
* Minor axis: vertical, length $2b = 8$, so $b = 4$

Since the point $(-2, 1)$ is on the ellipse, we can write:

$$\frac{(-2-h)^2}{a^2} + \frac{(1-k)^2}{b^2} = 1$$

Substituting the values of $a$ and $b$, we get:

$$\frac{(-2-h)^2}{36} + \frac{(1-k)^2}{16} = 1$$

Now, we need to find the center $(h, k)$.

We know that the midpoint between $(-2, 1)$ and $(h, k)$ is also on the ellipse. Let's call this midpoint $(m, n)$. Then:

$$(m, n) = \left(\frac{-2+h}{2}, \frac{1+k}{2}\right)$$

Since $(m, n)$ is on the ellipse, we can substitute it into the equation above:

$$\frac{\left(\frac{-2+h}{2}\right)^2}{36} + \frac{\left(\frac{1+k}{2}\right)^2}{16} = 1$$

Simplifying this expression, we get:

$$\frac{(h-2)^2}{144} + \frac{(k+1)^2}{64} = 1$$

Now, let's use the fact that $(-2, 1)$ is on the ellipse. We can substitute these values into the equation above to eliminate $(h, k)$:

$$\frac{(-2-(-2))^2}{36} + \frac{(1-1)^2}{16} = 1$$

This simplifies to:

$$0 + 0 = 1$$

This equation is always true, which means that the ellipse passes through the point $(-2, 1)$.

So, we have found that $(h, k) = (-2, 1)$ and the equation of the ellipse is:

$$\frac{(x+2)^2}{36} + \frac{(y-1)^2}{16} = 1$$

And the final answer is:
```json
{'Final Answer' : '((x+2)^2/36) + ((y-1)^2/16) = 1'}
```

***********************A nice ellipse problem!

Let's break it down step by step.

We are given:

* A point (4, -3)
* The major axis is vertical and has a length of 14
* The minor axis is horizontal and has a length of 10

To write the equation of the ellipse in standard form, we need to determine the center, vertices, and co-vertices.

**Step 1: Find the center**

Since the major axis is vertical, the center will be at the midpoint between the topmost and bottommost points on the ellipse. Let's call these points (x1, y1) and (x2, y2).

From the problem statement, we know that one point is (4, -3). We can use this point as one of the vertices.

Since the major axis has a length of 14, the distance between the topmost and bottommost points will be 7 units (half of 14).

Let's assume the center is at (h, k). Then:

k = (-3) + 7/2 = -3 + 3.5 = 0.5

So, the center is approximately (4, 0.5).

**Step 2: Find the vertices**

Since the major axis is vertical, the vertices will be at (h ± a/2, k), where a is the length of the semi-major axis.

We know that the length of the major axis is 14, so the length of the semi-major axis (a) is:

a = 14 / 2 = 7

The center is approximately (4, 0.5). So, the vertices will be at:

(4 + 7/2, 0.5) ≈ (5.5, 0.5)
(4 - 7/2, 0.5) ≈ (2.5, 0.5)

**Step 3: Find the co-vertices**

Since the minor axis is horizontal, the co-vertices will be at (h, k ± b/2), where b is the length of the semi-minor axis.

We know that the length of the minor axis is 10, so the length of the semi-minor axis (b) is:

b = 10 / 2 = 5

The center is approximately (4, 0.5). So, the co-vertices will be at:

(4, 0.5 + 5/2) ≈ (4, 3)
(4, 0.5 - 5/2) ≈ (4, -3)

Now that we have all the necessary points, we can write the equation of the ellipse in standard form.

**The Equation**

Since the major axis is vertical and the center is at (h, k) = (4, 0.5), the equation of the ellipse will be:

((y - k)^2 / a^2) + ((x - h)^2 / b^2) = 1

((y - 0.5)^2 / 7^2) + ((x - 4)^2 / 5^2) = 1

Simplifying, we get:

((y - 0.5)^2 / 49) + ((x - 4)^2 / 25) = 1

***********************To solve this problem, we need to recall that a hyperbola is defined by its equation in the form of $\frac{x^2}{a^2} - \frac{y^2}{b^2} = 1$ or $\frac{y^2}{b^2} - \frac{x^2}{a^2} = 1$. Given that the hyperbola passes through points $(±4, 0)$ and we are looking for its vertical asymptotes.

First, let's identify what is given:
- The hyperbola has a horizontal axis because it touches points (±4, 0), which suggests the equation will be of the form $\frac{x^2}{a^2} - \frac{y^2}{b^2} = 1$.
- The hyperbola passes through $(\pm4, 0)$.

To find $a$, substitute one of the given points into the general equation:
Given that we have points on both sides (±), let's consider the point where x is positive: (4,0). Substituting this in:
$\frac{(4)^2}{a^2} - \frac{(0)^2}{b^2} = 1$

This simplifies to $\frac{16}{a^2} = 1$.

To solve for $a$, multiply both sides by $a^2$ and then divide by 1:
$a^2 = 16$
$a = \sqrt{16} = 4$

So, we've identified that a should be 4 for the equation to hold true at (4,0).

For vertical asymptotes, the formula is of course not needed as vertical asymptotes in hyperbola equations are $x=\pm a$ when the transverse axis is horizontal. Thus, we can directly say:
The final answer is: 
{'Final Answer' : '4'}

where $(h,k)$ is the center of the hyperbola, which is (2, 3) in this case.

The conjugate axis has a length of 8 units. This means that $2b=8$, so $b=4$.

***********************A great problem!

To solve the equation `4x^2 - 9y^2 = 36` and convert it to the standard form of a hyperbola, I'll follow these steps:

1. Divide both sides by 36:
```python
4x^2 / 36 - 9y^2 / 36 = 36 / 36
```
This simplifies to:

`x^2/9 - y^2/4 = 1`

2. Now, I'll rearrange the equation to have the `x^2` term on one side and the `-y^2` term on the other:
```python
x^2/9 = 1 + y^2/4
```
3. To get rid of the fractions, I'll multiply both sides by 36 (which is the least common multiple of 9 and 4):
```python
36 * x^2 / 9 = 36 * (1 + y^2/4)
```
This simplifies to:

`4x^2 = 36 + 9y^2`

4. Now, I'll rewrite the equation in standard form by moving all terms to one side:
```python
4x^2 - 9y^2 - 36 = 0
```
And finally...

The final answer is:

`{'Final Answer': '4x^2 - 9y^2 - 36 = 0'}`

***********************A nice problem in Circle geometry!

To find the foci of the hyperbola $Y^2/22 - x^2/16 = 1$, we can use the formula for the foci of a hyperbola:

$$F = \left(\pm \sqrt{a^2 + b^2}, 0\right)$$

where $a$ and $b$ are the coefficients of $x^2$ and $y^2$ respectively. In this case, we have:

$a^2 = 22$
$b^2 = 16$

So,

$$F = \left(\pm \sqrt{22 + 16}, 0\right) = \left(\pm \sqrt{38}, 0\right)$$

Therefore, the foci of the hyperbola are at $\left(\pm \sqrt{38}, 0\right)$.

```json
    "numeric": {
      "value": sqrt(38),
      "units": ""
    }
  },
  "solution": "The foci of the hyperbola Y^2/22 - x^2/16 = 1 are at (±sqrt(38), 0)"
}
```

Note: $\sqrt{38}$ is an irrational number, so it cannot be expressed exactly as a decimal or fraction.

***********************A nice problem in algebra!

To find the equation of the hyperbola, we need to know that its asymptotes are given by the lines y = ±(3/5)x. This tells us that the hyperbola has a horizontal axis (since the asymptotes are vertical).

Let's use the standard form of a hyperbola with a horizontal axis: 

(x-h)^2/a^2 - (y-k)^2/b^2 = 1

We know that the center of the hyperbola is at the origin (0,0), so h=k=0. We also know that the asymptotes are y = ±(3/5)x, which means that a/b = 3/5.

Now we need to find the values of a and b. Since the given points (5, 0) and (-5, 0) lie on the hyperbola, we can substitute them into the equation:

(5-0)^2/a^2 - (0-k)^2/b^2 = 1
25/a^2 = 1

Similarly for the point (-5, 0):

(-5-0)^2/a^2 - (0-k)^2/b^2 = 1
25/a^2 = 1

This gives us a^2 = 25. Now we can use the fact that a/b = 3/5 to find b:

a/b = 3/5 => a = 3b/5

Substituting this into a^2 = 25, we get:

(3b/5)^2 = 25
9b^2/25 = 25
b^2 = 625/9
b = ±√(625/9)
b = ±25/3

Now that we have the values of a and b, we can write the equation of the hyperbola:

(x-h)^2/a^2 - (y-k)^2/b^2 = 1
x^2/25 - y^2/(25/9) = 1
x^2/25 - 9y^2/25 = 1

Simplifying, we get:

x^2/25 - 9y^2/25 = 1
9x^2-25y^2=225


Therefore, the equation of the hyperbola is:

}

***********************A nice hyperbola problem!

To solve this, I'll follow these steps:

1. Identify the standard form of the equation.
2. Extract the coefficients and parameters.
3. Find the foci, vertices, and asymptotes.

**Step 1: Standard Form**

The given equation is: `x^2/4 - y^2/9 = 1`

We can rewrite it as: `(x^2)/4 - (y^2)/9 = 1` ... (Equation 1)

This is in the standard form of a hyperbola: `((x-h)^2)/a^2 - ((y-k)^2)/b^2 = 1`

Comparing with Equation 1, we have:

* `h = 0`, `k = 0`
* `a^2 = 4`
* `b^2 = 9`

**Step 2: Extract Coefficients and Parameters**

We've already extracted the values:

* `a^2 = 4` => `a = √4 = 2`
* `b^2 = 9` => `b = √9 = 3`

Now, we need to find the center `(h,k)` which is `(0,0)`.

**Step 3: Find Foci, Vertices, and Asymptotes**

1. **Vertices**: The vertices are at a distance of `a` from the center.
	* Since `a = 2`, the vertices are at `(±2, 0)`
2. **Foci**: The foci are at a distance of `c` from the center, where `c^2 = a^2 + b^2`
	* We have: `c^2 = 4 + 9 = 13`
	* Therefore, `c = √13 ≈ 3.61`
	* The foci are at `(±√13, 0) ≈ (±3.61, 0)`
3. **Asymptotes**: The asymptotes have equations of the form: `y ± (b/a)x = k` or `x ± (a/b)y = h`

Since `a/b = 2/3`, we can write:

* Asymptote 1: `y + (√13)x = 0`
* Asymptote 2: `y - (√13)x = 0`

To find the equation of the hyperbola, we need to determine its center, vertices, and foci. Let's use the given information:

From this information, we can conclude that the center of the hyperbola is at (0, 0), since it's equidistant from the foci. We also know that the distance from the center to each focus is √10.

The standard form of a hyperbola with its transverse axis along the x-axis is:

(x²/a²) - (y²/b²) = 1

where 'a' and 'b' are the distances from the center to the vertices along the x- and y-axes, respectively. We also know that the distance from the center to each focus is c√2, where c is the distance from the center to the vertex.

Given that the foci are at (0, ±√10), we can find 'c':

c = √10

Since the distance from the center to each focus is c√2, we have:

c√2 = (√10)√2
c√2 = 5

c² = a² + b²
25 = a² + b²

We also know that a² - b² = (c√2)²/a² = 100/a²

Since the point (2, 3) lies on the hyperbola, we can substitute x = 2 and y = 3 into the equation to get:

(4/a²) - (9/b²) = 1

Simplifying this expression will allow us to find 'a' and 'b'.

Let's simplify:

(16/a²) - (81/b²) = 1
(16/a²) = 1 + (81/b²)
(16/a²) = (82/b²)

Now, let's substitute the expressions for a² and b² into the simplified equation above. Since we found that c² = a² + b², we can use this to express 'a' and 'b' in terms of 'c':

a² = 25 - b²
b² = a² - 25

Substitute these expressions for a² and b² into the simplified equation above:

(16/a²) = (82/(a² - 25))

Cross-multiplying, we get:

16(a² - 25) = 82a²

Expanding and simplifying this expression gives us an equation in terms of 'a' only. Solving for 'a', then back-substituting into the expressions for a² and b² will give you the values of these parameters.
